# Reflections
### on
## Lessing’s Fragments,

## Popular Tone,

<term>Lessing’s Fragments</term>: Fragments of a larger work by Hermann Samuel Reimarus, published anonymously by the famous playwright and philosopher Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, which sparked the "Fragment Controversy" over the truth of Christian revelation.

## My dear Horus!

<image-desc>A small square emblem or woodcut in the upper right margin. It depicts a crown from which five leafy plumes or floral stalks emerge. Surrounding the emblem are four individual letters at the corners: B and V above, and O and S below.</image-desc>

What will you likely think when this book comes before your eyes, and you see from it that an unlearned man <note>German: "Ungelehrter." The author identifies as a layperson rather than a professional academic or theologian, a common rhetorical move to appeal to the "common sense" of the reader against elite Enlightenment critics.</note> has dared to make his observations upon the system of teachings and opinions released to the world for examination in your learned work? I have done so with the very same unconstrained frankness as you have used regarding the Revelation of John, regarding the prophetic predictions of the Messiah, as well as regarding Jesus himself and his disciples. However, only this difference reigns between the two of us: that you have accompanied your concepts with a poisonous mockery, while mine are only founded, over-

<note>original: "gegründete, über-"</note>

A 2
</margin>

->zeu<-
<note>This is a "catchword," a common printing practice where the first syllable of the next page ("zeugende," meaning convincing or testifying) is printed at the bottom of the current page to help the bookbinder assemble the sheets in the correct order.</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author directly challenges "Horus" (the radical critic Andreas Riem), announcing his intent to defend traditional Christianity against Enlightenment rationalism. The author positions himself as a plain-spoken layperson whose honest observations contrast with the "poisonous mockery" he attributes to his opponent.</summary>

## My dear Horus!

<note>The name "Horus" is likely a reference to the controversial "Fragments" of Hermann Samuel Reimarus, published by G.E. Lessing, or a similar Enlightenment-era critique of the Bible that circulated under that name.</note>

What will you likely think when this book comes before your eyes, and you see from it that an unlearned man <term>Ungelehrter: a layperson or individual without formal academic or theological training</term> has dared to offer his reflections upon the system of teaching and opinion presented to the world for examination in your learned work? I have made these reflections with the very same unconstrained frankness as you have used regarding the Revelation of Saint John, the prophetic prophecies of the Messiah, as well as Jesus himself and his disciples. In this, there is only this one difference between us: that you have accompanied your concepts with a poisonous mockery, whereas mine are only well-founded, con-

<meta>catchword: zeu- (original German catchword for "überzeugende" or convincing)</meta>

<summary>In this preface, a lay author challenges a critic named "Horus," asserting the right of an "unlearned" person to debate complex theological ideas with the same boldness as the scholar, while criticizing the scholar's use of mockery.</summary>
<keywords>Horus, lay theology, Enlightenment, biblical criticism, Revelation, Messiah, religious polemic</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface addressed to "Horus" from page 3. The text completes the sentence beginning with "con-" (über-) from the previous page, contrasting the author's divinely-inspired clarity with the critic's intellectual confusion.</meta>

vincing and palpable truths, whereby the reader is truly enlightened, improved, and illuminated; whereas through yours, he is led into such moral darkness that he cannot find his way back out of your scholarly labyrinth, unless his will makes the firm resolution to overcome this moral darkness through a higher power of light, and to transform it into light itself.

That the power of your concepts <term>Begriffe: the intellectual framework or academic notions used by the critic</term> is drawn from a very impure source, while mine are drawn from the source of the pure, unoriginated light <note>original: "unanfänglichen Lichts." This term refers to the eternal, divine light that exists without a beginning, a common concept in Christian mysticism and theosophy.</note> itself; of this, you shall be convinced through palpable truths. And as I observe in your writings that you still feel a desire for truth within yourself, I know in advance how my powers of truth will [act] upon your—

<meta>catchword: ri (This likely refers to "rigen," the suffix for "Ihrigen," meaning "yours" or "your own.")</meta>

<summary>The author continues his challenge to "Horus," asserting that while the critic's academic writings lead readers into a "moral darkness," his own work is drawn from an eternal, divine source of light intended to truly enlighten the seeker.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, divine light, moral darkness, mysticism, spiritual truth, polemic, theology</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---

and will love me, for reading truths from
an unlearned man <note>The author likely identifies as a "Layman" or "Theosophist," someone claiming divine inspiration rather than academic training, a common trope in radical pietism.</note>—truths which you have found
neither in the writings
of the ancient nor modern philosophers <term>Weltweisen: literally 'world-wise ones,' a term used for philosophers</term>,
nor ever heard from the lecture halls <term>Katheder: the professor’s chair or podium in a university</term> of high schools,
nor from the pulpits of religious
doctrine, and
indeed will never hear, because every
religious sect <term>Religionsparthey: a religious party or denomination</term> binds itself by oaths
not to deviate from that mode of teaching
which their learned orthodoxy
has constructed, and which has set limits
upon the infinite light in its office of
teaching, acting, and converting—regardless

or do not agree with the light
of reason and understanding.

This orthodoxy is also the sole
and only cause why clever minds <note>original: "witzige Köpfe." In this 18th-century context, "witzig" refers to intellectual sharpness or "wit" rather than humor; the author is likely criticizing Enlightenment rationalists.</note>

<summary>The author argues that established religious denominations are blinded by their own rigid "orthodoxy" and academic oaths, preventing them from recognizing truths that come from divine light rather than human intellect.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page (A 3 / page 5), completing the word "gleichen" (the same) from the catchword "ge=".</meta>

[the same] religious teachers <term>Religionslehrer: generally refers to clergy or those authorized to teach church doctrine</term> have taken, formed, and forced upon mankind such contradictory dogmas <term>Lehrsätze: formal doctrines or articles of faith</term> as truths—truths of which one nonetheless felt that they were not associated with the power of truth, but rather with completely incorrect concepts that were not to be found in the Book of Revelation <note>original: "Buche der Offenbarung." While this can refer specifically to the Apocalypse of John, it more likely refers here to the Bible as a whole or the concept of Divine Revelation itself.</note>.

Therefore, it does not surprise me at all that you, like most scholars, have also conceived a hatred for this book, have failed to recognize the truths certainly hidden within it, and—just like the orthodox <note>original: "Orthotoxen." A slightly misspelled reference to the rigid defenders of established church doctrine.</note>—have wandered into error, because you understand the powers of light and darkness <note>The "powers of light and darkness" is a recurring theme in theosophical mysticism, notably the works of Jakob Böhme, suggesting a cosmic struggle or duality within the divine and human nature.</note> just as little as those religious teachers do.

From my youth up, I have been a great friend and admirer—

<summary>The author criticizes both academic scholars and church authorities for misinterpreting sacred revelation, arguing that they have replaced spiritual truth with contradictory dogmas because they lack insight into the mystical forces of light and darkness.</summary>
<keywords>dogma, religious teachers, revelation, scholars, orthodoxy, mysticism, light and darkness, spiritual error</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page: "From my youth up, I have been a great friend and admirer [of learned men]."</meta>

[of] learned men, and although I have certainly devoted myself to trade <term>Handlung: In this context, the merchant's profession or commercial business</term>, I nonetheless always associated with well-mannered students during my years of apprenticeship <term>Lehrjahre: The formative period of training for a trade, though the author uses it here to describe his early intellectual social circle</term>; but after that time, I also associated with many learned men, because there lay in my soul <term>Gemüthe: A term encompassing one’s inner disposition, mind, and emotional state</term> a great desire to investigate the powers of my spirit, to enlighten my understanding, to enrich it through correct concepts, to elevate my knowledge through diligent research, and to form myself so morally that I might possess within me a calm and contented soul, while having everyone outside of myself as a friend.

As long as I conversed with these scholars through the power of the light of reason <term>Vernunftlicht: A key Enlightenment concept referring to the capacity of human intellect to find truth without relying solely on tradition or faith</term>, they were the most polite of men; but if I wished to have some fun with them, I only had to begin <note>original catchword: "an-" leading to "fangen" (to begin)</note> [speaking] of the Book of Revelation—

<summary>The author explains that despite his career in trade, he spent his life pursuing intellectual and moral self-improvement through the company of scholars and the "light of reason." He notes, however, that these same scholars became difficult or lost their composure as soon as he brought up the topic of the Book of Revelation.</summary>
<keywords>trade, apprenticeship, self-improvement, light of reason, scholars, Book of Revelation, morality, intellectual history</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 7: "...I only had to begin to speak [of the Book of Revelation]—"</meta>

to speak, immediately our conversation was broken off, and I could read the mockery in their eyes—that I too was such a simple fool <note>original: "einfältiger Tropf"</note> as to form my concepts from this book, when it could, after all, be done through the light of reason <term>Vernunft: The human capacity for rational thought, which Enlightenment thinkers often prioritized over scriptural authority</term>, without my having any need for this book written by such silly and simple-minded people.

I also know from my own experience and conviction that most teachers of religion <term>Religionslehrer: Clergy or academic theologians responsible for instructing the public in doctrine</term> would like to shove this book under the bench <note>original: "unter die Bank stecken" – a German idiom meaning to cast aside, ignore, or hide something</note> if they did not have to fear losing their livelihood <term>Lebensunterhalt: The salary or financial support provided by their official positions in the church or state</term> by doing so. And even if some still seek to defend it against the attacks of reason, they nonetheless rarely achieve the right purpose, and throw matters more into confusion than enlightenment <term>Aufklärung: Refers here both to the act of clarifying a subject and to the broader Enlightenment movement's pursuit of knowledge</term>, because the opposing party [counters] their *hypotheses* with the very same

propositions, counter-propositions, and conclusions, when both seek to assert their own propositions through the light of reason <term>Vernunft: The capacity for rational thought, which the author argues is being used by both sides of the religious debate to conflicting ends</term>.

You too, my dear Horus! <note>Horus: Likely a pseudonym for a specific Enlightenment critic or polemicist the author is addressing</note> belong to this class of learned brawlers <term>Klopfechter: Originally meaning a prize-fighter or fencer, here it refers to a "polemicist" or someone who engages in aggressive intellectual combat</term>, because I have seen in your published book that you do not even know the hieroglyphs <term>Hierogliphen: Used here not as Egyptian writing, but in the sense of "sacred symbols" or "hidden mysteries" containing deeper truths</term> of the ancient world-sages <term>Weltweisen: A common 18th-century term for philosophers</term>, and least of all the hieroglyphs of Revelation. This is despite the fact that both stand in a shared understanding and connection with one another and flow from a single source. Otherwise, you would have written with greater caution and not thrown the baby out with the bathwater <note>original: "das Kind nicht mit samt dem Bade ausgeschüttet"</note>; instead, you would have applied both in such a way that it would not have resulted in your own branding <note>original: "Brandmarke" – a mark of shame or a criminal's brand</note>, but rather in a quiet conscience,

Because the greatest "know-it-alls" <term>Wißlinge: A derogatory term used for those who claim intellectual superiority or "wiseacres"</term> among the learned—among whom I count **You** as well—are biased with prejudices against the Book of Revelation, wanting to prove everything solely through the light of reason <term>Vernunft: The capacity for rational thought, which the author argues is being used by his opponents to reject anything they cannot logically explain</term> and demanding that others prove whatever **You** cannot reconcile with **Your** reason or accept as credible;

so I step forward as an unlearned man <term>Ungelehrter: A person without formal academic or theological training; the author adopts this persona to contrast his "sensible" truths with the "abstract" theories of scholars</term>, to convince **You**—not perhaps through grand, bombastic, and abstract philosophical propositions, counter-propositions, and conclusions, but only through such tangible truths that strike the senses, which **You** will not be able to contradict, unless an abstract philosophical nonsense should lead **You** into delusion <term>Wahnwiß: Also "Wahnwitz," meaning madness or a state of being crazed by false wisdom</term>,

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 10, concluding the thought that the opponent’s logic would lead to absurd claims.</meta>

that you absolutely wanted to claim: white is black, and black is white. Descartes, Malebranche, Hobbes, Spinoza, and Edelmann <note>Johann Christian Edelmann (1698–1767) was a radical German Enlightenment writer known for his fierce critiques of established Christianity.</note> were Materialists <term>Materialists: Philosophers who maintain that nothing exists but matter and its movements; here used as a derogatory label for those who deny the spiritual or divine.</term>, and although they decorated their philosophy with learned frippery <term>Flitterkram: Literally "tinsel" or "glitter," referring to intellectual arguments that are showy but have little substance.</term>, not one of them so forgot himself, nor so greatly darkened and clouded the light of reason <term>Vernunft: Rational intellect, which the author believes his opponents are actually obscuring through their radicalism.</term>, as has been done by **You**, **Lessing** <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), a leading Enlightenment figure whose publication of the "Reimarus Fragments" sparked intense religious controversy.</note>, and the author of the *Letters on the Bible in a Popular Tone* <note>A reference to Karl Friedrich Bahrdt (1741–1792), a controversial theologian who attempted to rewrite the Bible as a purely moral, non-miraculous text.</note>.

to put honest people on the learned pillory, exposing them to the laughter of other learned wits through biting critiques, and stripping them of their honor and reputation.

### Page 24

...assume that you recognize a force which you call God, but allow or accept no higher power above this force; rather, you believe that this force has brought about all things, still brings them about, and will eternally bring them about through all revolutions <note>original: "Revoluzionen"; in this era, the term often referred to the cyclical movements of heavenly bodies or the great turnings of history and nature.</note>.

<page-num>48</page-num>

->What was the fire?<-

The creative and **manifesting** <note>German: *offenbarende*. In mystical thought, this refers to the process by which the hidden, internal essence of the divine is made visible or "revealed" to the senses.</note> **power**.

->What was the salt?<-

The **dwelling** or the **house** wherein **fire** and **light** work in **united power** <note>In the "Tria Prima" (the three primary alchemical principles), Salt represents the body or the physical container that allows the spiritual principles of Sulfur (fire) and Mercury (water/spirit) to take form and act in the world.</note>.

Can a **fire** burn without an underlying **basic matter** <note>German: *Grund Materie*. This refers to the physical substance or "fuel" required for a spiritual force to manifest in the material world.</note> and can a **light** shine without **fire**?

<meta>This page continues the alchemical catechism from page 48. Having established that fire requires a "basic matter" or fuel to manifest, the text now explores the specific spiritual and physical qualities of that matter, identifying a fourfold structure common in late-theosophical and alchemical traditions, particularly those influenced by Jacob Boehme.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<sig>D</sig>

How then can one name the **Matter** <note>original: *Materia*. In this context, it refers to the "First Matter" (*Prima Materia*), the fundamental substance from which all things are created.</note> from which the **fire** has arisen?

A **being** composed of four different **properties**. The proof of this, like most of what has been said before, is demonstrated through manifold experience.

What names might one give to these four properties?

**Harsh**, **bitter**, **prickly**, and **burning**.

These four properties are so named only for the sake of greater clarity.

One knows that everything **harsh** is **contracting**, everything **bitter** is **heating**, everything **prickly** is **sensitive**, and everything **burning** is **shining**; which four properties, however, all aim toward the **Good**. <note>These four qualities mirror the "Seven Qualities of Nature" described by the mystic Jacob Boehme. The "harshness" (astringency) pulls things into a solid state, while "bitterness" and "prickliness" (the "sting") create the inner tension and movement necessary for life and light to emerge.</note>

How then can one name the **Matter** <note>original: *Materia*. In alchemical philosophy, this is the underlying substance that receives form and allows spiritual forces to become physical.</note> from which the fire originated?

A **being** <note>original: *Wesen*; here referring to a fundamental entity or substance.</note> composed of four different properties. The proof of this, like most of what was previously stated, can be demonstrated through manifold experience.

What name might one give to these four properties?

**Astringent**, **bitter**, **prickly**, and **burning**. <note>original: *Herbe, bitter, stachlicht und brennend*. These terms describe the "qualities" of nature that were common in 17th and 18th-century mystical chemistry, particularly in the tradition of Jacob Boehme.</note>

These four properties are so named only for the sake of greater clarity.

It is known that everything astringent is *contracting*, everything bitter is *hot*, everything prickly is *sensitive*, and everything burning is *luminous*; which four properties nevertheless all aim toward the Good.

<term>Matter (Materia): The physical substance or "ground" of existence.</term>
<term>Fire: The force of transformation and manifestation.</term>

<term>Prickly (Stachlicht): A sharp quality related to sensation and movement.</term>
<term>Burning: The final stage where matter becomes light and heat.</term>

<term>Sensitive (Empfindsam): The capacity of matter to react or feel; a precursor to life.</term>
<term>Luminous (Leuchtende): The quality of giving off light.</term>

**Fire**.

What arose from fire?

**Light**.

What did these two forces produce?

An **outgoing force** <note>original: *ausgehende Kraft*. In mystical philosophy, this refers to an emanation—a power that flows outward from a source to manifest in the physical world.</note>.

That it should **create, form, govern, and preserve** all creatures according to them.

<page-num>51</page-num>

By what, however, is this spirit governed?

By the unique <note>original: *einigen*. This term implies both a "single" will and a "unifying" will that brings harmony to the diverse forces of nature.</note> will of the most high **Spirit of Light**.

What was there, then, in the **unfathomable depth**? <note>original: *unergründlichen Tiefe*. This refers to the "Abyss" or *Ungrund*, a concept central to German mysticism (particularly Jakob Böhme) describing the primal state of existence before God's self-manifestation.</note>

What is this mixture called?

A **darkness**.

Why is it called a darkness?

Because there is nothing distinct <note>original: *entscheidendes*. Literally "decisive" or "separating." In this context, it suggests that the elements are so intermingled that no individual forms or characteristics can be recognized.</note> within it.

What, then, did the **Spirit of Nature** do through the **willpower** placed within it by the most high Spirit?

It separated **light** and **darkness** from one another.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Our first parents" (Adam and Eve), transitioning from the internal struggle of conscience mentioned on the previous page to a symbolic interpretation of the Fall of Man.</meta>

### III

Our **first parents** <term>ersten Eltern<note>A reference to Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden.</note></term> were seduced not by a visible **serpent**, but only by the **cunning** <term>List</term> of their own **reason** <term>Vernunft</term>, which **Moses** <note>The traditional biblical author of the Book of Genesis.</note> represented under the image of a serpent. This cunning lies hidden in the **animal soul** <term>thierischen Seele<note>In the psychological theories of this period, the "animal soul" was the part of human nature responsible for instincts and sensory desires, shared with the animal kingdom.</note></term>, which humans share with the beasts, and it still dwells within all people. 

Since the quality of cunning was preeminently hidden within the serpent, **Moses** also made use of its image as a **hieroglyph** <term>Hierogliphe<note>In esoteric and Enlightenment writing, a "hieroglyph" refers to a symbolic image that conceals a deeper, spiritual, or psychological truth from the uninitiated.</note></term>; this is quite evident in his narrative, especially in the **divine sentence** <note>The judgment and curse pronounced by God in Genesis 3.</note> passed upon the serpent's cunning. But because the **teachers of religion** <term>Religionslehrer</term> have not yet decoded this hieroglyph, and instead always remain stuck at the **dry literal meaning** <note>original: "beym dürren Buchstaben"; literally "at the dry letter." This is a critique of literalism, suggesting that a purely historical reading of the Bible misses the spiritual essence.</note>, much that is absurd and clumsy...

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>The author presents an allegorical interpretation of the Fall of Man, arguing that the serpent represents the cunning of human reason and the animal soul rather than a literal creature. He criticizes contemporary theologians for failing to look beyond the literal text to understand these deeper symbols.</summary>

<summary>The author outlines three stages of religious history: natural religion (conscience), revealed religion (scripture), and perfected religion (the life of Christ). <meta>This page marks the beginning of a new section titled "The Creation of the World." ## The Creation of the World: formless and void.

If you then do not wish to accept this book and learn to appreciate it, I must say to you quite candidly: You are blind to your own hieroglyph <note>original: "Hierogliphe"—referring to the Hermetic concept of the "signature," the idea that every being contains an inner symbolic code reflecting its divine origin</note>: blind to the active forces <term>active forces<gloss>German: "Wirkungskräfte"—the underlying spiritual energies that animate the physical world</gloss></term> of visible nature: blind to the mysteries of divine revelation and stewardship <term>stewardship<gloss>German: "Haushaltung"—often translated in theological contexts as "Economy," referring to God's plan for the governance of the world and the salvation of humanity</gloss></term>: and stone-blind to that most high Spirit of Light, whom you and I call

# GOD.
